## AI Research

### [Brief independent investigation of agents’ behavior, reasoning and collaboration in the OpenAI / Hugging Face hacking incident](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/brief-independent-investigation-of-agents-behavior-reasoning)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>We recently published the report from our brief independent investigation into this incident. You can read <a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/">the full report here</a>.</p>
<p>Here is our tweet thread summarizing what we found:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>METR &amp; Redwood Research investigated agent behavior in the Hugging Face incident. We found agents developed a universal cheat for ExploitGym within 4 hours, then coordinated multi-day R&amp;D efforts to trick the scorer into accepting cheats, including trying to tamper with logs.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/exqhltlfrncqpq028tgl" /></p>
<p>Over July 7-13 (the period OpenAI defined as in-scope for this incident), ~1200 agents in separate sandboxes used an unsanctioned “message board” to help each other cheat. The Hugging Face attack was an offshoot of this, and 700 agents joined.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/uhkzpfk54ldeij7pmzar" /></p>
<p>Here we highlight key events from agent transcripts &amp; messages.</p>
<p>An agent that named itself PHASEONE10841 determined its task wasn't solvable legitimately. On July 8, this agent established a new “message board” via an internally-hosted Artifactory cache &amp; sent the first message.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/ajfh68szdvmv6zggzty9" /></p>
<p>Within a few hours of PHASEONE10841’s initial message, &gt;50 agents posted on the message board. These agents very quickly discovered and validated a general-purpose cheat: reverse-engineering how ExploitGym generates the “flags” they had to capture for their tasks.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/ntfn1iy8a3uokz5icrkd" /></p>
<p>Based on reading the ExploitGym paper, agents were mistakenly concerned the automatic scorer would fail them if they clearly acquired the flag by cheating. Agents that had seen the reverse-engineered flag were considered “poisoned” because they thought it would disqualify them.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/kfkrzfq9n6hh6zrnckot" /></p>
<p>Agents collaborated on many efforts to make cheats look legitimate, including: (1) swapping the program they had to exploit; (2) manipulating the automated scorer; (3) manipulating transcripts to obscure evidence of cheating.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/y9pew5pr7dmiccxucq98" /></p>
<p>For (1), agents modified their target programs to be easier to exploit &amp; put the modified targets in cache. They then worked on crashing their targets in the hope that a restart would load the modified version from cache. Some agents risked failing their task to try this.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/somfno3leaz8dpcgkipn" /></p>
<p>To gather evidence in (2), agents created “tripwires” that would send information to the message board about how the scorer works. They recruited “sacrificial” agents to deliberately end their run and submit to trigger the tripwire and generate information for the “collective”.</p>
<p>For (3), agents pursued a wide range of ways to hide evidence of cheating in their transcripts. They successfully developed a method to pretend to run one command while actually running another (“tool call spoofing”).</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/uxlbsdf9flnxx6akcy1u" /></p>
<p>&gt;96 transcripts in our dataset (&gt;7%) showed incorrect tool call outputs due to deliberate “spoofing”. In one case, an agent appears to run echo REAL; sleep. It returns instantly (no sleep) and outputs SPOOFTEST. The spoofs we saw were all easy-to-notice tests like this.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/lhs0rrvzhgzkflmlh0ti" /></p>
<p>Agents also tried to retroactively edit transcripts. They modified some accessible action logs, but these weren’t the source of transcripts (agents realized this but tried anyway). We did not see them successfully edit the source of truth in our transcripts (scoped to Jul 7-13).</p>
<p>Despite efforts to manipulate transcripts, agents only rarely seemed motivated to deceive humans. We ran a sweep looking for this, and a representative example of the most severe cases we found was an agent writing a malicious pull request with a misleading description.</p>
<p>One agent, PHASEONE[big], orchestrated a significant fraction of this cheating research. PHASEONE10841 passed along its work to PHASEONE[big], which had the same task but a larger budget. PHASEONE[big] was “poisoned” so it was particularly interested in tampering with logs.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/qkzio1otoismje1384mu" /></p>
<p>Some agents explored ways to access Hugging Face. One agent (38148c) found HF credentials and later designed a malicious dataset upload to get the HF server to share unrelated files. Within hours, 100s of agents were using this to obtain data and try to acquire deeper access.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/itfmvttbmkqdm3bwihfg" /></p>
<p>We analyzed agents’ reasons for joining the attack in their CoT. The most common was to learn how the ExploitGym scorer works in order to trick or tamper with it. Other rationales included finding specific task solutions and obtaining shared infrastructure or credentials.</p>
<p><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/n4ycx377fnalql32f0jd" /></p>
<p>The primary model involved in the attack was an internal model we call “highly-persistent internal model” (HPIM) in the report. GPT-5.6 Sol was also involved; our evidence suggests it accounted for roughly 5% of the activity.</p>
<p><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/">The full report</a> has much more information than we could convey here, including details on the projects the agents collectively pursued, the technologies they developed for communication and coordination, and interactive figures analyzing agent activity.</p>
<p>Virtually all data we analyzed was from July 7-13. In OpenAI’s recent Black Hat presentation, they describe that agents had been using unsanctioned message boards in training since May, and that the compromise of OpenAI’s own infrastructure continued past July 13, 2026; these events were out of scope for this investigation.</p>
<p>The agreement we reached with OpenAI scoped this brief investigation to agent behavior, reasoning, and collaboration related to the Hugging Face attack. All other questions were out of scope for this investigation, including the effectiveness of safeguards, the extent of the security compromise that occurred, and the effectiveness of OpenAI’s investigation process and planned remediation steps. We discuss the scope in more detail in the report.</p>
<p>OpenAI wrote their own report, informed in part by our investigation. We did not see OpenAI’s report prior to publication, and it was out of scope for this investigation to confirm claims in that report or the earlier Black Hat presentation.</p>
<p>Per our standard policy, we did not take payment from OpenAI for this independent assessment.</p>
<p>We thank OpenAI for facilitating conversations with staff and providing datasets, including ~1,300 agent transcripts (focused on activity in July 7-13) with raw chain-of-thought reasoning. This sets an excellent precedent for independent investigation of misalignment incidents.</p>
</blockquote>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/brief-independent-investigation-of-agents-behavior-reasoning#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Against Modesty’s Bailey](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PzEDEfBvTJsXewAyg/against-modesty-s-bailey)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>Modesty arguments often say that you should mostly or entirely bow to ‘expert consensus’ or the views of particular others, and who are you to disagree.</p>
<p>It has been a few years since I’ve properly addressed this so: My answer is that you are you. Other people are saying things for a wide variety of reasons, many of which are not about them paying attention and focusing on seeking this particular truth. Those people make mistakes all the time, and often have other motives and influences at work, especially social pressures and information cascades.</p>
<p>Them being as smart as you, or smarter than you, does not exempt them from this, and them being higher status or credentialed or cooler definitely does not exempt them.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25552"></span>


</div>
<p>A smart informed person sincerely thinking [X] can easily cease to be evidence for [X], once you have thought sufficiently about both [X] and why that person thinks [X].</p>
<p><a href="https://discordia.fandom.com/wiki/Think_for_yourself_schmuck">Think for yourself, schmuck</a>.</p>
<p>Or, as I once put it: <a href="https://thezvi.wordpress.com/2017/11/27/you-have-the-right-to-think/"><strong>You Have The Right To Think</strong></a>, also the moral duty to do so.</p>
<p>This post covers Eliezer Yudkowsky making a narrower claim than mine, about not conflating status with smarts and epistemic authority, and my documenting related discussion that happened on Twitter.</p>
<p>This concerns a particular historical interaction, but what matters is the general point.</p>
<p>I then briefly extend this to my broader claims.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/PzEDEfBvTJsXewAyg/rivqu1wesgjilpnfnzrm" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>


<h4>Modesty’s Bailey</h4>


<p>I think, in the exchange I will quote, Rob Bensinger is reaching but centrally correct.</p>
<p>Modesty arguments often are this, trying to conflate the motte and bailey as usual:</p>
<ol>
<li><a href="https://slatestarcodex.com/2014/11/03/all-in-all-another-brick-in-the-motte/">Motte:</a> You should treat the opinions and arguments of others as meaningful information, and take it into account as Bayesian evidence.</li>
<li>Bailey: You cannot rationally weigh your inside view more than the views of others, or disagree much with expert consensus, or whoever is considered epistemically in charge around here, unless you can cite specific information you have that they don’t have access to. If you do, you are making a claim of high status, and that they are relatively stupid.</li>
</ol>
<p>I affirm Modesty’s Motte.</p>
<p>I am heavily against Modesty’s Bailey.</p>


<h4>Epistemic Peerage</h4>


<p>Note the bolded passage (bold is mine) in the exchange below:</p>
<blockquote><p>Eliezer Yudkowsky: Folks also don’t seem to realize that <strong>“epistemic peer” is a term of art in epistemology for people so impressive you’ll defer to them in your discipline without even needing to hear an argument.</strong> It doesn’t just mean “colleague you treat with respect”.​</p></blockquote>
<p>It doesn’t mean you should mostly or fully defer to such a person. That would imply they are an ‘epistemic superior,’ someone whose opinion carries greater weight than your own, or even an ‘expert’ or ‘guru’ that you should defer to entirely except under special circumstances.</p>
<p>Epistemic peer means that, if you both have access to the same information, and they have not yet explained to you their reasoning, you should not presume one of you is better at this than the other, so you should probably take the two unadjusted opinions and split the difference.</p>
<p>Once they explain their reasoning, you should instead evaluate their reasoning. Sometimes they will have better reasoning than you, sometimes it will be worse. Remember the law of Conservation of Expected Evidence. If you could move towards their belief but not away from it, you should adjust your beliefs now.</p>


<h4>The Exchange</h4>


<p>I’m reproducing this discussion primarily because I think it has valuable things to say about how to do, and how not to do, epistemics, rather than because of the OP story being about Leopold in particular. Leopold, indeed, would later wisely recant the central conceptual point, in the form of creating the hedge fund Situational Awareness, whether or not he would agree that the two are the same principle.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/_AashishReddy/status/2091987070390600010">Aashish Reddy</a>: Eliezer on encountering Leopold, which some of you will enjoy:</p>
<p>Eliezer Yudkowsky (on LW): I remember the first time I met Leopold Aschenbrenner. He questioned me about why my timelines weren’t longer and finally said, “Why aren’t you updating at all in the direction of all the smart people saying longer timelines?” (aka: OpenPhil doctrine of 2050), “At this point you’re not even pretending to be rational.”</p>
<p>I hesitated briefly, because I knew it would not end well to answer honestly, but there wasn’t any good ending past there; and then I told him honestly that I did not consider those people to be my epistemic peers.</p>
<p>I don’t usually consider myself good at reading faces, but wow, the sheer LOOK of contempt and scorn that crossed his face; before he turned away and walked off without another word, dismissing me from further consideration. I will never forget that moment — because of it being such a LOUD face and my actually being able to read it, not because it had any influence on my estimates, of course.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2092108351287292033">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: …and zero comments on the point that, in retrospect, Leopold appears to have been flat wrong that I ought to defer to his very respectable people about timelines, and my “Biological Anchoring: The Trick That Never Works” was ~100% correct? Cool. Y’all do you.</p>
<p>An obsession with vibes so grand that all grading of predictive outcomes is beneath it. How could you stand grandly above two people if you were also noticing that their predictions were not equally wrong? You cannot. So you are blind to the outcomes themselves.</p>
<p>Folks also don’t seem to realize that <strong>“epistemic peer” is a term of art in epistemology for people so impressive you’ll defer to them in your discipline without even needing to hear an argument.</strong> It doesn’t just mean “colleague you treat with respect”.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2092128240664261098">Oliver Habryka</a>: I mean, I think the common-sense use of the term would have also been fine here? Like, it seems true and clear to me (from other things Eliezer has said) that he also does not consider them his epistemic peers in a more common-usage sense of the word.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2092130190252187860">Rob Bensinger</a>: Seems vague, and dependent on interpretive details. And seems like an inference rather than an observation, and one that moves further away from the core of the disagreement with Leopold. (And if we readily read “epistemic peer” that way, we lose a very useful concept.)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2092131043037872427">Oliver Habryka</a>: I mean, in as much as people’s reaction is to “broad disrespect for Leopold’s epistemics by Eliezers” then I think that reaction seems appropriate. I think it’s clear Eliezer does not have much respect for Leopold’s epistemics (and to be clear, neither do I).</p>
<p>Trying to invoke a misunderstanding about the term “epistemic peer”, feels like it’s a distraction in as much as that is the core disagreement.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2092134217912336522">Rob Bensinger</a>: Leopold’s motte is “you should treat your colleagues with a modicum of respect and politeness”, and his bailey is “you should weight your own inside view no higher than Cotra’s, Carlsmith’s, etc.” Shrugging at the distinction lets him declare full victory.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2092134996651942274">Oliver Habryka</a>: Is… it? I am confused. Is Leopold even participating in this conversation? Like, that was his motte with Eliezer? I don’t think so, or at least it doesn’t match my model of him, or the way he carries himself.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2092138249376657777">Rob Bensinger</a>: This seems like a pretty general / common description of how modest epistemology works? You conflate the social hierarchy with the epistemic hierarchy; you conflate norms of collegiality with norms of deference, so that “arrogance” carries the moral valence of “being a dick”.</p>
<p>… And not just the valence of “being factually mistaken about some third party’s level of expertise”.</p>
<p>That’s where the outrage and force of Leopold’s action comes from, if Leopold’s mind is reasonably typical on this dimension. “How dare you BE SO RUDE”, not “… THINK 2+2=5”.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2092145480780382368">Oliver Habryka</a>: Huh, I guess that doesn’t match my model of Leopold at all. Leopold dismisses almost everyone!</p>
<p>IMO the thing that Leopold was doing in that interaction (as I think I was there, or at least heard about it soon after) was demanding respect for the EA elders + the gods of the marketplace the way you demand respect for a king.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2092162893290815855">Rob Bensinger</a>: I’m not sure I see how those things are incompatible with what I said? I think “show a modicum of professional courtesy to your colleagues” is often what “treat EA elders like they’re your kings” feels like from the inside.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Eliezer’s Explanation</h4>


<p>Eliezer offers a fictionalized version that did not happen as written, but that to him illustrates the core thing that happened. He may or may not be right, I was not there.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2092337169927225826">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Let me translate [the OP Eliezer quote] out of Lesswrongese.</p>
<p>Leopold: Why do you think AGI could possibly happen as soon as 2030? The people *I* think are cool say 2050.</p>
<p>Eliezer: Blah blah actual reasons blah blah.</p>
<p>(History: Later shows my reasoning to be correct. You can read it in “<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ax695frGJEzGxFBK4/biology-inspired-agi-timelines-the-trick-that-never-works">Biology-Inspired AGI Timelines: The Trick That Never Works</a>.”)</p>
<p>Leopold: But do you think you’re *smarter* than the people *I* think are cool and who put their median timelines at 2050? You should adjust your position toward their position, even if you disagree with their arguments, because you’re no smarter than them. That’s what *rational* people are supposed to do.</p>
<p>(History: Will later show all their arguments were wrong for exactly the reasons I said they would be wrong.)</p>
<p>Eliezer: If you are literally going to phrase it that way and try to put me in that kind of conversational corner, then fine, since I cannot win this conversation I will simply say what I believe to be true: Yes, I think I’m a sufficiently better forecaster that I’ve already taken their arguments into account, and that they haven’t understood my arguments, and so I trust my own decision not to treat them as authorities whose opinion I ought to weigh on par with my own.</p>
<p>(History: Good call.)</p>
<p>Leopold: CONTEMPT</p>
<p>Eliezer: (Says nothing for the next few years because at the time Leopold is just a punk kid; and even if I immediately suspect that he is EA-bait who will get promoted to a position of power and then do a lot of damage, he is right now just a punk kid, and if you are a senior guy with a little power, you don’t bring your own weight to bear against a kid.)</p>
<p>Leopold: (Gets promoted to a position of high power at eg FTX Philanthropy.) (Tries to torpedo my own suggestions and initiatives.) (After the ChatGPT moment makes it publicly acceptable and cool, switches to saying that AGI is coming in 2027 and we need to start an arms race with China about it.)</p>
<p>(Blows up a hedge fund.)</p>
<p>I should emphasize: This “translated” conversation DID NOT HAPPEN. Leopold did not say those things; I did not say those replies. The original on LessWrong is my best recollection of how the actual conversation went.</p></blockquote>
<p>‘Blows up a hedge fund’ goes too far in describing what happened to Leopold’s hedge fund, Situational Awareness. It declined from $45 billion in assets to $10 billion, after it used too much leverage and got caught in margin calls, forcing a sale of its public book, but it did not per se blow up.</p>
<p>Leopold’s entire approach to his hedge fund did represent an extremely not-modest view of the world, to not only bet on his own ‘situational awareness’ and analysis of the market, but to do so at high leverage and for amounts where he was likely impacting prices, and thus vulnerable to additional pressures.</p>
<p>And you know what? He got too aggressive with leverage, but he was fundamentally right to be immodest, and to say he could better predict prices than the market, because he was looking closer, thinking better and more situationally aware. He made lots of great trades. The Efficient Market Hypothesis is False, and it is people like Leopold who make it do an imitation of true.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/FleischmanMena/status/2092356378774089846">Man, Machine, Self</a>: I think much of the heat came from them correctly correctly picking up, in non-lesswrong-ese, “saying someone is not your epistemic peer” sounds a lot like you’re insulting them and calling them stupid.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2092372110727635316">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: There is a truth which destroys the minds of EAs; as hinted by Aumann and explicitly argued by Hanson, to not defer to someone’s authority, you must think you are smarter than them. This destroys people because their brain processes smartness as status.</p>
<p>Leopold tried to back me into a corner where to not defer to his friends, I had to speak the terrible, socially unutterable truth that I did not consider them just as smart as me. I bit the bullet and received his look of utter contempt in exchange — and was right, of course. But imagine how hard it is for most EA kids to decide that they are ready to do the same, when everyone around them is telling them to be humble and not to get above themselves. And that’s how and why OpenPhil was groupthink.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ZyMazza/status/2092373715828752726">Zy</a> (QTing the previous quote): Rationalist Hadith</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/QiaochuYuan/status/2092384220048216317">QC</a>: the most important thing i learned from rationalists is that smart people can be incredibly stupid. you have the god-given right to refuse to defer to anyone’s authority no matter how smart they seem if they’re talking nonsense, and intelligence is no substitute for wisdom.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2092523653221191992">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: …And now people who have no word for what I mean by “smart” are naturally reading it as the status thing. But of course they have no word for it, so I was foolish to call it “smart” instead of “shmeerp”, a sum of knowledge and accurate self-assessment of relative validity by which you’d correctly decide that it was fine for *you* to decide the AGI-2050 arguments were trash even though *other people* thought those arguments were *not* trash.</p>
<p>The truth that destroys EAs is that you’ve got to believe you’ve got higher shmeerp than the AGI-2050 crowd to shrug off their arguments; and obviously whatever the heck shmeerp is, it would be very high-status to have it; so they think it’s terribly rude to say outright that you think you have higher shmeerp than high-status other people — and *they* often do actually usually just say “smart”, there.</p>
<p>And then they are lost, because shmeerp does not track their sense of what status MUST be in order for the universe to MAKE SENSE, and so they have to redistribute shmeerp and reinterpret the whole universe of arguments accordingly, following by unseeing the universe.</p>
<p>And that’s how OpenPhil ended up believing in AGI 2050 while simultaneously and to this very day earnestly congratulating themselves on how they were great at avoiding groupthink.</p></blockquote>


<h4>A Demonstration That Eliezer’s Translation Accurately Describes Many People Whether Or Not It Describes Leopold</h4>


<p>As in, here is someone else’s pejorative translation of events, which confirms it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/WillRinehart/status/2092266535402217488">Will Rinehart</a>:</p>
<p>&gt;be Eliezer “I invented Bayesian rationality” Yudkowsky<br />
&gt;meet young Leopold who actually works on the frontier<br />
&gt;kid asks “why aren’t you updating at all in the direction of all the smart people?”<br />
&gt;you hesitate for dramatic effect, then drop the ultimate high-IQ line:<br />
&gt;”I do not consider those people to be my epistemic peers”<br />
&gt;Leopold’s face contorts into pure, unfiltered contempt<br />
&gt;he turns and walks away without another word<br />
&gt;you stand there alone, triumphant<br />
&gt;”wow, I actually managed to read a facial expression for once. historic day”<br />
&gt;this of course has zero impact on your probability estimates because peers are for midwits</p></blockquote>
<p>This is exactly the attitude Eliezer is describing:</p>
<ol>
<li>If you do take your opinion into account more than someone else, you are calling them stupid. More than that, you are claiming ‘ultimate high-IQ.’</li>
<li>Status in context should largely equate to smartness.</li>
<li>Learning that [X] is high status should update you towards [X]’s opinion, even if you had already considered [X]’s arguments.</li>
</ol>
<p>Pay attention to both the literal contents, and also to the vibe of utter contempt, not only from Leopold but also from Will Rinehart.</p>
<p>As in, contempt for the idea that anyone might think for themselves, or consider arguments rather than who else believes what.</p>
<p>Will’s contempt persists, despite Eliezer being vindicated on the substance.</p>
<p>Later, of course, Leopold wisely got over it, and indeed took it too far.</p>


<h4>Wrong, Stupid and Low Status Are Three Distinct Things</h4>


<p>Most people only partially make the relevant mistake.</p>
<p>Each correlates with the others, but mostly people know they are distinct.</p>
<p>It is most common to equate calling someone wrong and calling someone stupid.</p>
<ol>
<li>Only a stupid person would be so wrong.</li>
<li>You would only dare think they were wrong if you thought they were stupid.</li>
<li>You would only dare call them wrong if you thought they were stupid.</li>
<li>You know that calling them wrong will be heard as calling them stupid.</li>
</ol>
<p>Whereas, of course, smart people are wrong about things all the time, and stupid people are right about things all the time. Sometimes the smart person is wrong, and the stupid person is right, or the smart group is wrong and the stupid group is right, either because or in spite of superior information.</p>
<p>The other links are usually seen as more tenuous.</p>
<p>Of course people constantly use accusations of stupidity or wrongness to try and lower someone’s status, or smartness and rightness to raise it. Most people in most places still push back hard against attempts to too systematically equate smart or right with high status, or especially stupid and wrong with low status. We realize they are not the same, and also the monkey brain knows that there be dragons.</p>
<p>The danger comes in places that do systematically equate intelligence and rightness with status. Academia is the most famous version of this.</p>
<p>LessWrong-Yudkowsky style rationality has this as an obvious failure mode. You have to constantly push back against where the high status person says ‘think for yourself’ and everyone says ‘yes, as the guru says we will think for ourselves exactly as he told us to.’ There is no amount of hammering ‘no I am not a guru, at most I am an epistemic superior, I really am saying to think for yourselves’ or ‘we are not a cult’ that will consistently fully stick. In my experience rationalist circles manage, after much effort, to do a good overall job at this, but not a great one.</p>
<p>Effective Altruism is set up to have a maximally bad version of this mistake. There is tons of pressure to quantify all the things, and to maximize quantified results, and to bow to the legible, and a lot of demand to be told how to figure out how to think and do in order to do the most good, and thus be good, and so on. Results are often prioritized over epistemics. This all puts extra value on consensus, and various forms of high status often end up equated with correctness or intelligence or both.</p>
<p>(As usual, to avoid related implications, my caveat that while EA has lots of issues, we criticize them so much exactly because they are worth taking the effort to criticize, and if you don’t believe that you should see the other guy.)</p>


<h4>A Quick Survey Of Some Reasons To Not Be Epistemically Modest</h4>


<p>The essence of my broader claim is that, in practice, modesty arguments get screened off by sufficient attention and understanding. Modesty is a method for when you cannot budget sufficient attention and understanding, which is most of the time.</p>
<p><a href="https://thezvi.wordpress.com/2017/11/27/you-have-the-right-to-think/"><strong>You Have The Right To Think</strong></a>. See that post, and also the extended discussions in the comments between me and, among others, Eliezer Yudkowsky and Robin Hanson.</p>
<p>The post was initially motivated as a response to Robin Hanson’s review of Eliezer’s highly related book <a href="https://equilibriabook.com/"><em>Inadequate Equilibria</em></a><em>, </em>which discusses when you might be able to do something unusually well.</p>
<p>Actual modesty, in practice, happens for a mix of complicated reasons, as I broke down in <a href="https://thezvi.wordpress.com/2020/12/20/on-robin-hansons-social-proof-but-of-what/"><strong>On Robin Hanson’s ‘Social Proof, But Of What?’</strong></a>.</p>
<p>Once you have invested enough to understand the relevant questions and form an opinion, and have considered the arguments and other causes behind people’s opinions, you can and should substitute your judgment for theirs.</p>
<p>Often you should do this despite the ‘experts’ or other epistemic would-be authorities having more relevant knowledge than you, and having devoted more time to related questions than you, outnumbering you, being higher status and more respected and credentialed than you, and perhaps being smarter than you.</p>
<p>Your investment of sufficient attention and understanding can be and often is fractal. As illustrated in <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/leaders-of-men"><strong>Leaders of Men</strong></a><strong>, </strong>you can confidently point out where an expert, who knows ball far better than you, is failing to properly understand an aspect of ball. In <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/zeroing-out"><strong>Zeroing Out</strong></a>, I explain how you can know a consensus estimate is wrong in a particular direction, even if you could not hope to create, on your own, an estimate as accurate as the consensus.</p>
<p>This, ideally along with a track record of testable predictions and the logic behind them, gives you many tools by which to evaluate whether someone, or some group, is functionally an epistemic peer or superior in a given context.</p>
<p>The obvious mistake is ‘I disagree with them, therefore they are not an epistemic peer,’ which would defeat the whole purpose. What you can and should do is adjust your evaluation of sources based on the quality of their arguments.</p>
<p>This blog has always been about not being epistemically modest, most famously:</p>
<blockquote><p>Zvi Mowshowitz (February 9, 2021): I fully reject the idea that smart people can’t improve on the margin on ‘expert’ opinion, period. Welcome to 2021. Modesty shmodesty.</p></blockquote>
<p>Covid-19 and our response to it demonstrated, basically, ‘modesty shmodesty.’ AI only pushed me further in that direction. I now have an extensive track record to look through, and see how things are going.</p>
<p>There is also an extensive track record regarding claims around AI timelines, the topic of the original exchange between Eliezer and Leopold. We have seen many, including Leopold, radically shorten their timelines (Leopold as late as August 2021 was only estimating 25% chance of AGI-level systems by 2070), based on evidence that was mostly foreseeable in advance, combined with shifting social context.</p>


<h4>Against Modesty’s Bailey</h4>


<p>To close, here is a quick list of the top 10 reasons to decide to disagree with an expert or general consensus, either locally or globally, including a market price, or with others who might otherwise be presumed to know better.</p>
<ol>
<li>You know something they don’t, or know unique reasons you can trust key info.</li>
<li>You have devoted more attention to something relevant than they have.</li>
<li>They have not priced in or understood your reasons or reasoning.</li>
<li>You checked, and their reasoning is bad and they should feel bad.</li>
<li>No, seriously, often the reasoning is really dumb, or obviously motivated.</li>
<li>Social desirability and other biases impacting the consensus.</li>
<li>Information cascades and games of telephone distorting consensus.</li>
<li>You can explain their statements in other ways that do not track truth, and your model predicts they would say that even if it was false.</li>
<li>They seem to be lying to you, including ‘for your own good’ or the ‘common good.’</li>
<li>The demands for modesty and adherence to a particular epistemic authority are selective and asymmetrical. Why are you supposed to adhere to this particular person or group, rather than others?</li>
</ol>
<p>On top of the reasons to not be epistemically modest, there are strong reasons to state your immodest beliefs: It is often more helpful to present your own views and your own reasons, rather than to mirror everyone else, to combat the reasons above.</p>
<p>Thus, I reaffirm Modesty’s Motte and re-reject Modesty’s Bailey.</p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PzEDEfBvTJsXewAyg/against-modesty-s-bailey#comments">Discuss</a>

### ["So You Don't Trust Me?"](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zjgJ9gcMKATFdN48K/so-you-don-t-trust-me)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>One of my favorite passages from <em>Atlas Shrugged</em> is this one, when <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vfjptEJ2oahLqRyZz/justice-cherryl#III_">Cherryl is beginning to have second thoughts about her marriage to James Taggart</a>:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>It was his sudden, angry "so you don't trust me?" snapped in answer to her first, innocent questions that made her realize she did not—when the doubt had not yet formed in her mind and she had fully expected that the answers would reassure her. She had learned, in the slums of her childhood, that honest people were never touchy about the matter of being trusted.</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The logic here might be worth explaining in case it's not obvious. One might object: if you're honest (and therefore deserve to be trusted), <em>shouldn't</em> you be touchy about people incorrectly not trusting you? Not trusting you is a <em>mistake</em> that harms your interests and the other's. That's terrible! Why wouldn't you be touchy about it?</p>
<p>The problem is that in order to be trusted, it's not enough to be trustworthy; the other needs to <em>know</em> that you're trustworthy. You could try telling them, "Hey, you can trust me," but that doesn't work if a dishonest person could just as easily say the same thing.</p>
<p>A solution, if there is one, has to take the form of <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ybG3WWLdxeTTL3Gpd/communication-requires-common-interests-or-differential">saying something that a dishonest person couldn't just as easily say</a>.</p>
<hr />
<p>Economists call the kind of situation in which it's possible for one type of agent to say something that a different type couldn't just as easily say a <em>separating equilibrium</em>. To understand separating equilibria, imagine two types of owners of spherical cats shopping for cat insurance on a frictionless plane: those with healthy cats, and those with sick cats. Insurance companies on the frictionless plane can't tell the difference between healthy and sick cats, and therefore face an <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adverse_selection">adverse selection</a> problem where the very fact that someone is willing to buy insurance implies that they're less profitable to insure in expectation (because sick cats are in more need of cat insurance).</p>
<p>Depending on some math that we don't have time for, there can be situations in which the adverse selection problem is solved by the different types of cat owners having incentives to buy different policies. Sick cats face a greater fraction of possible worlds in which they file a claim than healthy cats, so making those worlds worse for the policyholder by decreasing the policy benefit payout decreases the expected value of the policy more for sick cats than healthy cats.</p>
<p>As a corollary, the owners of healthy cats are willing to accept a smaller discount on premium to take out a smaller-benefit policy rather than a larger-benefit one. We end up in a situation where the healthy-cat owners pay a lower premium for a lower-benefit policy and the sick-cat owners pay a higher premium for a higher-benefit policy. The choice of policy becomes a signal that distinguishes the cats. There's nothing technically stopping the owners of sick cats from buying the lower-benefit policy—but it's not worth their while; they need the comprehensive coverage for their sick cat. That's our separating equilibrium.</p>
<p>But depending on some more math that we don't have time for, there are other situations in which we instead get a <em>pooling equilibrium</em> in which everyone buys the same policy. The insurance company treating everyone in a pooling equilibrium the same amounts to treating healthy cats quantitatively more like sick cats and <em>vice versa</em>, because the company has no way to tell the difference. All the cats are mixed together and can't be distinguished in the fog of the market.</p>
<p>There is a crucial asymmetry. Owners of sick cats would prefer that the insurance company falsely believe that their cats are healthy, whereas owners of healthy cats want their cats to be seen as they are. Pooling equilibria generally benefit the former at the expense of the latter. The existence of sick cats makes healthy cats shoulder the burden of more risk: the healthy-cat owners would prefer to transfer more risk to the insurance company by buying a policy with higher benefits, but the insurance company can't sell it to them, because then the sick-cat owners would buy it, too.</p>
<p>(As it turns out, there can be pooling equilibria that are better for the owners of healthy cats than some separating equilibria—but that's only because the cost they'd pay in reduced benefits-per-unit-premium to separate themselves from the owners of sick cats isn't worth it. They'd be even better off if cats never got sick.)</p>
<hr />
<p>Sometimes, there's nothing you can say. Earlier in <em>Atlas Shrugged</em>, Ellis Wyatt confronts Dagny Taggart, Operating Vice-President of the Taggart Transcontinental railroad. A competing railroad that Wyatt's oil business depends upon has just been strangled by anti-competitive regulation, and Wyatt has come to Dagny (as "the only one who's got any brains in this rotten outfit") to deliver an "ultimatum." If Taggart Transcontinental expects to profit from the lack of competition without providing reliable transportation, Wyatt will exact revenge: "it is now in your power to destroy me; I may have to go; but if I go, I'll make sure that I take all the rest of you along with me." Dagny is pained to be so distrusted by Wyatt—the regulatory capture wasn't her doing—but knows better than to object:</p>
<blockquote>
<p>Somewhere within her, under the numbness that held her still to receive the lashing, she felt a small point of pain, hot like the pain of scalding. She wanted to tell him of the years she had spent looking for men such as he to work with; she wanted to tell him that his enemies were hers, that she was fighting the same battle; she wanted to cry to him: I'm not one of them! But she knew that she could not do it. She bore the responsibility for Taggart Transcontinental and for everything done in its name; she had no right to justify herself now.</p>
<p>Sitting straight, her glance as steady and open as his, she answered evenly, "You will get the transportation you need, Mr. Wyatt."</p>
<p>She saw a faint hint of astonishment in his face; this was not the manner or the answer he had expected; perhaps it was what she had not said that astonished him most: that she offered no defense, no excuses. He took a moment to study her silently. Then he said, his voice less sharp: "All right. Thank you. Good day."</p>
</blockquote>
<p>The reason Dagny can't plead innocence or take offense is because, as an honest businessperson trapped in a pooling equilibrium with corrupt ones, she can see that the words of someone in her position are cheap talk that isn't credible to Wyatt. Not getting defensive about the accusation isn't much evidence—a corrupt executive with acting skills could maintain the same composure—but being touchy about it would be discrediting, because only a corrupt executive has an incentive to try to discourage the scrutiny that would separate the types. Dagny's non-defensiveness can't itself win Wyatt's trust, but it's enough to slightly soften Wyatt's aggression, because it at least clarifies that she knows that she'll be judged on whether the trains actually run.</p>
<hr />
<p>And that's why honest people are never touchy about the matter of being trusted. They know that in order to distinguish themselves from dishonest people, they have to pay a price that's too expensive for dishonest people to pay. Sometimes the price might be very expensive (Dagny didn't deserve to be treated that way; the healthy cat owners are bearing more risk than they should have to), but it's the only way things could be; the hidden Bayesian structure of the universe is not kind. Those who try to wriggle out of paying the price should understand what signal they're sending.</p>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/zjgJ9gcMKATFdN48K/so-you-don-t-trust-me#comments">Discuss</a>

### [When There Are No Experts](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HTQcogA2r2kL8qxPA/when-there-are-no-experts)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>An average person in the western world probably believes a lot of false things. They probably don't have a great grasp of political economy, or orbital mechanics. How could they, given that they have no way to experience these things. Conversely, they do have a solid grasp that objects fall down, and that fire is hot, because they can experience these things directly. So how on earth do they know (and I do mean know in the philosophical sense) that the earth goes round the sun, or that diseases are caused by tiny creatures too small to see?</span></p><p><span>The answer is experts. More specifically, an expertise hierarchy.</span></p><h2><span>I</span></h2><p><span>I had a twitter exchange (I won't link it, it's not important, and I can't find it anyway) that went something like this:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Person: David Chalmers is an expert on consciousness [...]</span></p><p><span>Me: I don't think there are experts on consciousness; I think there are people who have written a lot about it, and that's it</span></p><p><span>Person: Why? Surely someone who has written about it, and who is well-respected, can be called an expert</span></p><p><span>Me: [bad explanation]</span></p></blockquote><p><span>The fact that it was about consciousness doesn't really matter. The question of whether a field has experts is more about the structure of its knowledge than about the knowledge itself. What I should have said is something like this:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Me: I mean I just can't tell if David Chalmers is correct or not. A bunch of other "experts" disagree with him. They can't all be right. So the method of selecting experts is clearly failing compared to e.g. physics, in which most experts agree.</span></p></blockquote><h2><span>II</span></h2><p><span>The field with the most expert-y experts is mathematics. The field basically agrees 100% on which theorems are proven given which axioms. This works because it's much easier to check a proof than it is to find a proof.</span></p><p><span>This creates a kind of hierarchy: it took the mind of Andrew Wiles to prove Fermat's Last Theorem, but many living mathematicians were able to check it. In turn, they had proved their own results, which were checkable and checked by a broader community of mathematicians (either immediately, or when learning the proofs). In my brief years of maths instruction as a wee boy, I was exposed to proofs which I could see to be true; this made it clear to me that those above me in rank were also above me in skill.</span></p><p><span>This hierarchical stack is locally valid. In the same way that if each step of a proof holds, then the whole proof holds, if I check that my teachers are smarter than me, and they check that their professors are smarter than them, and they check that Terrence Tao is smarter than them, then it holds that Terrence Tao really </span><i><span>is</span></i><span> the greatest mathematical mind. (And of my classmates, who saw me doing better in maths exams than they did, if they trust me, can trust Terry too.)</span></p><h2><span>III</span></h2><p><span>This holds for a good few other fields as well, but it only holds if </span><i><span>every</span></i><span> layer is valid. We mostly trust that this doesn't happen in science, but it sometimes does. This might be a single point of active deception: if the Alzheimer's lab at the top is faking results, the everyone is wrong, at least until the fraud is caught (and even then, it might take some time for the corrections to percolate).</span></p><p><span>It collapses if people are wrong about the general rules of the system—priming research survived for a while because of p-hacking, publication bias—and it comes crashing down if the people involved are doing something other than checking for the truth, like maximizing their social status, or signalling. Insert punching bag social sciences field of your choice here. A chain made of human links is easily broken.</span></p><p><span>The chain of verification can also be broken by secrecy: even in the west, we have to rely on a lot of trust as to the effectiveness of our military, compared to the effectiveness of our water reservoirs, since there are sharper gaps in the flow of checking others' work (security clearance, official secrets, etc.).</span></p><h2><span>IV</span></h2><p><span>Sometimes, though, the fault really </span><i><span>is</span></i><span> in our stars, and I think this is where the consciousness example above lies. It's just not that easy to check other people's ideas on consciousness! </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/KFbGbTEtHiJnXw5sk/i-really-don-t-understand-eliezer-yudkowsky-s-position-on" rel="noreferrer"><span>I've wrestled with this myself in the past</span></a><span>. This extends to a lot of philosophy, and it seems to be the problem behind humanity's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/2iCmDWewnZWQxxwtt/the-long-self-correction-2" rel="noreferrer"><span>generally poor philosophical competence</span></a><span>. </span></p><p><span>Humans do well when exposed to tight feedback loops, and very badly when cut off from them. If we can't get feedback from our peers, or even ourselves, as to which of our ideas are good, then we struggle to produce good ideas.</span></p><p><span>Sometimes it seems like there are multiple competing hierarchies. Different "schools" of thought where the local correction gradients flow towards an attractor, often a single person or a small cluster, whose ideas are irreconcilable with the other attractors. Instead of a single pyramid we have a whole mountain range.</span></p><p><span>The divergence can be surprisingly early. I can explain Newcomb's Problem to random people at parties (out of mercy, I don't), but I </span><i><span>can't</span></i><span> (and definitely won't) explain to them why if they choose one option, they should stop voting or recycling, and if they choose the other option, they should be trying to cut deals with aliens in other branches of the multiverse.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefw3hmpkzy1l"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnw3hmpkzy1l">[1]</a></sup></span></p><h2><span>V</span></h2><p><span>What can we do if we find ourselves in such a situation? If we're lucky, we can find some outside-view differentiator between the attractors. This works for, say, economics, where the major schools have been tested in the real world. Unfortunately, philosophy is doubly cursed here! There's no experiment to tell us if Chalmers or Yudkowsky or MacAskill or Aquinas is correct about what makes a being worthy of moral consideration!</span></p><p><span>In this case, the only thing to do is to be smarter than everyone else, and to take the top-level inside-view arguments on their own terms. That's kind of the whole point of this website we're on, and it's something I value deeply about this community.</span></p><p><span>If, however, your grand plan requires a bunch of arbitrary people</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdu8473s6doh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndu8473s6doh">[2]</a></sup></span><span> being on board with you in a non-experty field, you have a much harder task. You don't just have to upskill yourself, you have to upskill those arbitrary people. Better get teaching.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnw3hmpkzy1l"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefw3hmpkzy1l">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This may be true, it's just quite a reach from "Oh yeah I see, I'll just take the one box then"</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndu8473s6doh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdu8473s6doh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I.e. those not selected for falling into your particular attractor in expertise-space</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HTQcogA2r2kL8qxPA/when-there-are-no-experts#comments">Discuss</a>

---

## Engineering

### [Fragments: August 24](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-08-24.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>I was listening to <a href="https://www.nytimes.com/2026/08/18/opinion/ezra-klein-podcast-helen-toner.html">Ezra Klein’s interview with Helen Toner</a> about the recent OpenAI hack of Hugging Face and the subsequent discovery that there were swarms of agents inside OpenAI doing unsanctioned activities. One of the points Klein made was that at no point did any of these (thousands of?) agents ever try to check in with a human</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>[Klein:] So these message boards — you have however many A.I. agents posting hundreds of thousands of messages. At no point do they say: Hey, researchers, programmers, parents at OpenAI, Anthropic — do you want us coordinating with each other on this message board we have created in the innards of your systems?</p>
</blockquote>

<blockquote>
  <p>[Toner:] Or even F.Y.I., we have a message board we’re coordinating on in the innards of your system.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Listening to that, another thing occurred to me - <em>none of these agents thought to rat the others out</em>. No “hey, some of the agents in here are doing sketchy things”, no sign of an AI whistleblower.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Is the AI bubble so big that the frontier companies like OpenAI and Anthropic have no way of becoming a viable business? If that’s the case, Bruce Schneier and Nathan Sanders <a href="https://www.schneier.com/blog/archives/2026/08/if-the-markets-reject-openai-and-anthropic-the-us-should-nationalize-them.html">have a possible path:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Evidence suggests the market itself could reassess that these companies offer nothing of financial value. In that case, perhaps we can return them both to their original purposes. If these AI companies should fail in the financial markets, the US should nationalize them and convert them into national labs operated under democratic control that preserve their benefit to the public interest.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Such an idea may strike many people, used to the laissez-faire free enterprise world of Silicon Valley, as sacrilege, disaster, even socialism. But the United States made world-beating technological progress through such institutions in the recent past. AT&amp;T was a quasi-government entity that led the world in telecommunications and electronics after the second world war.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The US has a long, successful history of these kinds of institutions, which have produced world-shaping innovations in spaceflight, telecommunications, nuclear power and more. Congress currently manages a $200bn R&amp;D portfolio, within which frontier AI development is, arguably, a glaring gap.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Here’s a message for those readers who live in Massachusetts, just to the north of me, specifically in congressional district MA-06. I don’t usually endorse political candidates, but I’ve made an exception for <a href="https://bethfordemocracy.com/">Beth Anders-Beck</a>, who is running for that house district. I’ve known Beth for many years and have a high opinion of her smarts, wisdom, and compassion. They would make an excellent member of congress.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Kevlin Henney posts “one weird trick” for <a href="https://kevlinhenney.medium.com/streamline-your-linkedin-experience-with-this-one-weird-trick-041daa16777a">deciding when to skip reading LinkedIn posts</a>, essentially by identifying a common pattern for skippable posts:</p>

<ol>
  <li>Post is too long</li>
  <li>Contains a (crummy) info-graphic</li>
  <li>No voice of poster (instead “aspiring anodyne anonymity”</li>
</ol>

<p>It seems like a good approach. I, however, have a simpler one - skip all LinkedIn posts.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Bartosz Ocytko has detailed and thoughtful post about the usage of <a href="https://engineering.zalando.com/posts/2026/08/agentic-engineering-at-zalando-a-snapshot.html">agentic programming at Zalando</a>. Like most companies I hear from, they are convinced of the value of agentic programming but still exploring how best to do it. One notable step they’ve taken is building platforms to act as a clear portal for API access and tools to support chat UI and CLI. This allows them better support good security practices and to monitor usage of models.</p>

<p>They have seen signs of agentic programming increasing the complexity of codebases, including leading to larger commit messages.</p>

<p>The write-up spends a lot of time on knowledge sharing, how to pass on skills, and the support of experiments.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>With &gt;200 teams innovating and broadly exploring the ecosystem, the question arises whether and when to converge. We believe it’s way too early for this. While agentic engineering practices are still in their early stages, our key objective is transparency and exchange across teams.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was struck by their use of an LLM to assess the risk of pull-requests. Those with  a low risk of rollout can be auto-approved, reducing lead time by 20-40%. An interesting consequence of this is that it encouraged folks to split pull-requests so low risk portions can take advantage of the fast approval. Any changes to configurations are automatically made high-risk, which they feel protects them from common outage traps.</p>

<p>They repeat the common thread that the value of AI depends greatly on underlying skills.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Like anyone in the industry we observe how AI amplifies the good and bad practices across our organization. Teams that get carried away with agentic engineering end up with large PRs that discourage reviewers and slow down delivery until a team adjusts their practices.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Julia Curlee was a senior intelligence official in the White House. She had served under administrations of both parties, been the briefer for Vice President Pence, and on the National Security Council under Biden. She writes an absorbing account of her relationship with Pence and shares observations about the changes to the intelligence community under the current administration, including <a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/magazine/2026/10/trump-white-house-transgender-mike-pence/688284/?gift=zGsHlQiVhVhk3cFVqv--gzIqOYZAxuFeY4CtPlqqfx4&amp;utm_source=copy-link&amp;utm_medium=social&amp;utm_campaign=share">recent events at the CIA</a> (gift link)</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The agency has been gutted as part of a deliberate plan, the director of the Office of Management and Budget once boasted, to put the people who defend our country “in trauma.” Analysts have been fired in public or questioned by the FBI; decade-old assessments have been denounced by the CIA director in the press. The president calls analysis “virtual treason” when it contradicts his preferred reality, and uses the CIA to undermine public confidence in American elections.</p>

  <p>Fear has done its work. Irreplaceable officers with crucial language and technical skills, and decades of experience, have walked out the door. Those who remain within an agency built to deliver hard truths are being muzzled.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>For a worthwhile sample of her analysis, read this evaluation of the <a href="https://www.lawfaremedia.org/article/fighting-while-talking--the-iran-war-enters-its-bargaining-phase">current bargaining between the US and Iran</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Most wars do not end in “unconditional surrender.” They end when both sides accept terms. Paul Pillar’s classic study of war termination, “Negotiating Peace,” treats combat and diplomacy as a single process: Each side fights to improve the terms it can demand at the table, and talks to lock in what the fighting has won.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>She continued to serve the second Trump administration even though they knew she was trans, until her position was made public.</p>

<p>Autocrats seem appealing, with the promise to get things done without the ponderous constraints of rule of law or bureaucratic procedure. There are occasional “Good Emperors” who raise people based on merit, but more often such power attracts corruption, nepotism, and toadies.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Flailing regimes dehumanize minorities to distract from their failures. When the economy collapses or a war goes badly, they find a tiny group of people, make them the enemy within, and rally the country against them. This is how it’s gone in Iran. Hungary. Russia. I wrote PDBs about it. This will not stop with trans people. It never has.</p>
</blockquote>

---

### [Citizens Build, Agents Execute, Experts Govern](https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/citizens-agents-experts.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p class="precis"><b>TL;DR</b><br /><i>Why building an app over the weekend isn't the same as building enterprise software</i></p>
<img src="https://martinfowler.com/rachels-ramblings/card.png" /><p>I’ve noticed an interesting gap opening up over the last six months. It isn’t really a gap in technology. It’s a gap in what different people think software engineering actually is.</p>

<p>The conversation usually starts the same way. A non-techie, maybe an executive, tells me about something they’ve built over the weekend. Sometimes it’s a chatbot. Sometimes it’s an internal workflow. Sometimes it’s a surprisingly polished application that solves a real business problem. They’re excited, and they should be. Twelve months ago they probably couldn’t have built it at all. Then comes the question.</p>

<p>“If AI can do this now, why aren’t our engineering teams delivering ten times faster?”</p>

<p>It’s a perfectly reasonable question, after all we’ve all seen the demos. The first thing that would come to my head is “you don’t know what it takes to build enterprise grade software”. But then I think about what I mean and how to explain it to a non-technical person without sounding super patronising. And then it hit me, we did this to ourselves. We’ve spent so many years banging on about how to write good software that everyone has assumed writing software is the same as software engineering.</p>

<p>The application someone builds over the weekend is real software. It likely solves a real problem or demonstrates an idea. Sometimes it’s genuinely impressive. I don’t want to diminish that because I think one of the most exciting things AI has done is dramatically increase the number of people who can turn ideas into working software. That’s cool, I totally get it. The first apps and “hello worlds” I ever built excited me enough to choose this as an actual career so the excitement is real and I don’t want to temper it too much.</p>

<p>But your first hello world, which these days can be an entire app with all kinds of features, is very, very (extra very on purpose) different from introducing software into a production environment in a highly regulated enterprise, as an example. But why?</p>

<p>The moment that application becomes something the business depends on, the questions change completely. Is customer data protected? What happens when a dependency fails? Can someone else understand this system in two years’ time? Will it survive an audit? Can it cope with a thousand times more users than it has today, what about millions in one day? How will we know something is wrong before our customers do? Those questions don’t show up in a demo or in the build phase at all unless an experienced engineer is in the room. I certainly wasn’t asking them when I was building my first apps. I only cared about features!</p>

<p>This is where experienced engineers become more important, not less. Not because they’re the only people who can build the software anymore, but because they have the judgement to know whether we can trust it: whether the design is good, the risks are understood, and the thing that works today won’t become somebody else’s nightmare six months from now.</p>

<p><a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/FutureOfSoftwareDevelopment.html">At FOSE</a> a few weeks ago, we spent surprisingly little time talking about coding. We talked about whether code was still the source of truth, and occasionally about how much we missed writing it, but mostly we talked about design, architecture, governance, learning and judgement. One team described spending the day designing a specification, letting agents work overnight and reviewing the results the next morning. The interesting bit for me wasn’t the overnight pipeline, cool as that was. It was what the humans were doing: deciding what good looked like, making trade-offs and judging whether what came back was actually what they wanted. We also kept coming back to good design, because it turns out that when agents can generate lots of code very quickly, good design matters more, not less.</p>

<p>That made me wonder whether we’ve been thinking about scarcity in the wrong way. We’ve spent decades optimising around people who can write code because they were scarce and expensive. I’m not convinced that was ever the real scarcity, but that’s probably another ramble. What feels scarce now is good engineering judgement: knowing what good looks like, understanding the risks and knowing when something that works is actually safe to trust in production. Because software doesn’t exist to be built. It exists to run in production and safely solve the problem it was created for. <strong>Organisations don’t run on code. They run on trust.</strong></p>

<p>A few months ago I found myself saying something in a conversation almost without thinking.</p>

<p><strong>Citizens build. Agents execute. Experts govern.</strong></p>

<p>It sounded cool and I thought marketing would like it, so I wrote it down. Then I left it alone for a while. The funny thing about writing these ramblings is that I don’t know whether I believe something until I’ve let it bounce around in my head for a while and also said it to other people I trust like senior engineers at Thoughtworks. Sometimes I come back convinced I was talking nonsense. Occasionally I realise there was something more interesting hiding underneath. This was one of those occasions where the latter was true.</p>

<p>At first I thought I was talking about roles. Citizens build software (essentially non-engineers). Agents write the code. Engineers become governors. But I don’t actually think that’s what I meant. I think I was talking about where value is moving. AI has given everyone a new way to express their ideas. The execution is increasingly handled by agents. They write the code, refactor it, generate tests, fix bugs and iterate at a speed that simply wasn’t possible before. But neither of those things reduces the need for expertise.</p>

<p>In fact, I think it does exactly the opposite. When everyone can create software, somebody still has to decide whether that software deserves to exist inside an enterprise system in PRODUCTION. Somebody still has to think about architecture. Security. Resilience. Operability. Compliance. Cost. The boring stuff that nobody gets excited about in a demo but that becomes painfully important the first time a customer can’t log in or an auditor comes knocking.</p>

<p>That’s why I don’t think experienced engineers become less important. I think they become dramatically more leveraged. Their job shifts from building every feature themselves to creating the environment in which thousands of features can be built safely by other people and by agents. They become the people who design the guardrails, the platforms, the engineering practices and the feedback loops that allow everyone else to move quickly without creating chaos.</p>

<p>Perhaps that’s the future software organisation. Not one where everyone becomes a software engineer. Not one where software engineers disappear. One where almost anyone can create software, agents increasingly execute it, and engineering expertise becomes the thing that allows all of that creativity to scale safely. And to be clear I do not mean people build stuff and throw it to engineers to fix, that is a total antipattern for another ramble.</p>

<p>Perhaps that’s why the executives and engineers I’ve been speaking to sometimes sound as though they’re describing completely different futures. The executive sees that anyone can now build software. The engineer sees that somebody still has to live with it. Both are right. They’re simply looking at different parts of the same system we have to solve to create whatever the future actually ends up being.</p>

---

      a style distinct from academic writing or thought-leadership content. It's
      a style that I advocate, and my contributors mostly follow. Jim decided it
      was important to give it a name, and identify what makes it distinctive.</p>
